\oldpage[III]{152}
$f_0$ is projective \sref[II]{II.5.5.4}.  Let $S=\bigoplus_{k\geq0}
\mathfrak{J}^k/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}$
be the graded $A$-algebra associated with the
$\mathfrak{J}$-adic filtration of $A$.  It is of finite type
because $A$ is Noetherian.  Put
\[
  \sh{S}'=f_0^*(\widetilde{S}),\qquad
  \sh{M}=\bigoplus_{k\geq0}\sh{M}_k.
\]
Then $\sh{S}'$ is a quasi-coherent $\sh{O}_{X_0}$-algebra of
finite type, and $\sh{M}$ is a quasi-coherent graded
$\sh{S}'$-module of finite type, since $\sh{F}_0$ is coherent and
generates $\sh{M}$ as an $\sh{S}'$-module.  Theorem
\sref{III.2.4.1},~\emph{(ii)}, therefore gives an integer $n_0$
such that, for $n\geq n_0$ and every $k$,
\[
  \HH^q(X_0,\sh{M}_k(n))=0
  \qquad(q>0).
  \tag{5.2.3.1}
  \label{III.5.2.3.1}
\]
Regarded now as an $\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}$-module,
$\sh{M}_k(n)$ consequently also satisfies
\[
  \HH^q(\mathfrak{X},\sh{M}_k(n))=0
  \qquad(q>0,\ n\geq n_0).
\]

Apply the exact cohomology sequence to
\[
  0\longrightarrow
  \mathfrak{J}^k\sh{F}(n)/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}\sh{F}(n)
  \longrightarrow
  \mathfrak{J}^h\sh{F}(n)/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}\sh{F}(n)
  \longrightarrow
  \mathfrak{J}^h\sh{F}(n)/\mathfrak{J}^{k}\sh{F}(n)
  \longrightarrow0.
\]
For $0\leq h<k$, $n\geq n_0$, and $q>0$, induction on $k-h$
gives
\[
  \HH^q(\mathfrak{X},
    \mathfrak{J}^h\sh{F}(n)/\mathfrak{J}^{k}\sh{F}(n))=0,
  \tag{5.2.3.2}
  \label{III.5.2.3.2}
\]
and in particular, for $h=0$,
\[
  \HH^q(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}_k(n))=0
  \qquad(n\geq n_0,\ k\geq0,\ q>0).
  \tag{5.2.3.3}
  \label{III.5.2.3.3}
\]
Another part of the exact cohomology sequence, with $h=0$, gives
\[
  \HH^0(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}_{k+1}(n))
  \longrightarrow
  \HH^0(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}_k(n))
  \longrightarrow
  \HH^1(\mathfrak{X},
    \mathfrak{J}^k\sh{F}(n)/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}\sh{F}(n))
  =0.
  \tag{5.2.3.4}
  \label{III.5.2.3.4}
\]
It follows that, for $h\leq k$, the canonical map
\[
  \HH^0(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}_k(n))
  \longrightarrow
  \HH^0(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}_h(n))
  \tag{5.2.3.5}
  \label{III.5.2.3.5}
\]
is \emph{surjective}.  Thus, for every $q$, the projective system $\bigl(\HH^q(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}_k(n))\bigr)\quad(k\geq0)$
satisfies condition (ML) when $n\geq n_0$.

Furthermore, every formal affine open $U$ of $\mathfrak{X}$ is an
affine open in each $X_k$ \sref[I]{I.10.5.2}.  Hence
$\HH^q(U,\sh{F}_k(n))=0$ for $q>0$ \sref{III.1.3.1}, and $\HH^0(U,\sh{F}_k(n))
\longrightarrow
\HH^0(U,\sh{F}_h(n))$
is surjective for $h\leq k$ \sref[I]{I.1.3.9}.  The hypotheses of
\sref[0]{0.13.3.1} are therefore satisfied.  For $n\geq n_0$ we
obtain:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[$1^\circ$] for every $q>0$, the map $\HH^q(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}(n))
\longrightarrow
\varprojlim_k
\HH^q(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}_k(n))$
    is bijective; hence, by \eqref{III.5.2.3.3},
    $\HH^q(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}(n))=0$;
  \item[$2^\circ$] the homomorphism $\HH^0(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}(n))
\longrightarrow
\varprojlim_k
\HH^0(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}_k(n))$
    is bijective.  Since the homomorphisms
    \eqref{III.5.2.3.5} are surjective, so is each homomorphism
    \[
      \varprojlim_k
      \HH^0(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}_k(n))
      \longrightarrow
      \HH^0(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}_h(n)).
    \]
\end{enumerate}
This proves both assertions.
\end{proof}

\label{III.5.2.4}
Under the hypotheses of \sref{III.5.2.3}, for every coherent
$\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}$-module $\sh{F}$ there is an integer $N$
such that, for $n\geq N$, $\sh{F}(n)$ is generated by its sections
over

\oldpage[III]{152}
$L''_{\bullet\bullet}$ is a projective resolution of
$P_\bullet$ in the category of bounded-below complexes of
$A$-modules \sref[0]{0.11.5.2.2}.  The assertion follows from

\begin{proposition}[6.5.13]
\label{III.6.5.13}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be two $S$-preschemes, and suppose that one of
the following conditions holds:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i)}] $X$ and $Z=X\times_SY$ are locally Noetherian, and
    $X$ is flat over $S$;
  \item[\textup{(ii)}] $S$ and $X$ are locally Noetherian, and $Y$ is of
    finite type over $S$.
\end{enumerate}
Let $\sh P_\bullet$ (respectively $\sh Q_\bullet$) be a
bounded-below complex of quasi-coherent $\sh O_X$-modules
(respectively $\sh O_Y$-modules).  Suppose in addition that, for
every $n$, $\sh H_n(\sh P_\bullet)$ (respectively
$\sh H_n(\sh Q_\bullet)$) is an $\sh O_X$-module
(respectively $\sh O_Y$-module) of finite type.  Then $\shTor_n^S(\sh P_\bullet,\sh Q_\bullet)$
is a coherent $\sh O_Z$-module for every $n$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $\sh P_\bullet$ and $\sh Q_\bullet$ are bounded below, the
spectral sequence
$''\sh E(\sh P_\bullet,\sh Q_\bullet)$ is biregular by
\sref{III.6.5.4}.  In either case (i) or (ii), $Z$ is locally
Noetherian, so \sref[0]{0.11.1.8} reduces the assertion to the
coherence of the terms $''\sh E_{pq}^2$.  The finite-type
hypothesis on the homology modules and formula
\sref{III.6.5.4.2} reduce the proposition to the special case in
which $\sh P_\bullet$ and $\sh Q_\bullet$ are concentrated in
degree zero, namely the following corollary.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[6.5.14]
\label{III.6.5.14}
Suppose that one of conditions \emph{(i)} and \emph{(ii)} of
\sref{III.6.5.13} holds, and let $\sh F$ (respectively $\sh G$)
be a quasi-coherent $\sh O_X$-module (respectively
$\sh O_Y$-module) of finite type.  Then
$\shTor_n^S(\sh F,\sh G)$ is a coherent $\sh O_Z$-module for
every $n$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The question is local on $X$ and $Y$, so we may suppose that
$S$, $X$, and $Y$ are affine.

\textnormal{(i)} Under the hypotheses of (i), $X$ and $Z$
are Noetherian.  There is therefore a locally free resolution
$\sh L_\bullet$ of $\sh F$ consisting of $\sh O_X$-modules of
finite type \sref[I]{I.1.3.7}.  Since $X$ is flat over $S$,
\sref{III.6.3.4} gives $\shTor_n^S(\sh F,\sh G)
=
\sh H_n(\sh L_\bullet\otimes_S\sh G)$.
The modules $\sh L_i\otimes_S\sh G$ are quasi-coherent
$\sh O_Z$-modules of finite type \sref[I]{I.9.1.1}, hence are
coherent.  It follows that
$\sh H_n(\sh L_\bullet\otimes_S\sh G)$ is coherent
\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.5.3.4}.

\textnormal{(ii)} Now suppose that condition (ii) holds.  Since
$A(Y)$ is a quotient of a polynomial $A(S)$-algebra in finitely
many indeterminates \sref[I]{I.6.3.3}, $Y$ is a closed
subprescheme of an affine $S$-scheme $Y'$ that is flat and of
finite type over $S$.  The scheme $Y'$ is Noetherian
\sref[I]{I.6.3.7}, so there exists a locally free resolution
$\sh M_\bullet$ of $j_*\sh G$ by $\sh O_{Y'}$-modules of finite
type, where $j:Y\to Y'$ is the canonical immersion.  By
\sref{III.6.5.12}, $\shTor_n^S(\sh F,\sh G)$ is the inverse image
under $1\times j$ of the $\sh O_{Z'}$-module
\[
  \sh H_n(\sh F\otimes_S\sh M_\bullet),
  \qquad Z'=X\times_SY'.
\]
As in (i), the $\sh F\otimes_S\sh M_i$ are coherent
$\sh O_{Z'}$-modules, and \sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.5.3.4} again gives the
conclusion.
\end{proof}

\begin{env}[6.5.15]
\label{III.6.5.15}
The theory developed above for two complexes
$\sh P_\bullet,\sh Q_\bullet$ of quasi-coherent sheaves on two
$S$-preschemes $X$ and $Y$ extends without difficulty to an
arbitrary finite family of $S$-preschemes $X^{(i)}$
$(1\leq i\leq m)$, each equipped with a complex
$\sh P_\bullet^{(i)}$ of quasi-coherent
$\sh O_{X^{(i)}}$-modules.  If $Z=X^{(1)}\times_SX^{(2)}\times_S\ldots\times_SX^{(m)}$
one thereby defines a quasi-coherent $\sh O_Z$-module $\shTor_q^S
\bigl(\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)},\ldots,
\sh P_\bullet^{(m)}\bigr)$.
We leave the development of the theory in this general case to
the reader, and record only, for later reference, the $E_2$-term
of the second, regular spectral sequence whose abutment is this
hypertor:
----- English, ega3/ega3-5.tex, page III 153 -----
\oldpage[III]{153}
$\mathfrak{X}$.  Equivalently, $\sh{F}$ is isomorphic to a quotient
of an $\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}$-module of the form
$(\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}(\setmin n))^k$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Since $X_0$ is Noetherian, the hypothesis on $\sh{L}_0$ and
\sref[II]{II.4.5.5} give an integer $n_0$ such that
$\sh{F}_0(n)$ is generated by its sections over $\mathfrak{X}$ for
$n\geq n_0$.  We may take $n_0$ large enough that $\Gamma(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}(n))
\longrightarrow
\Gamma(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}_0(n))$
is also surjective for $n\geq n_0$ \sref{III.5.2.3}.  There are
therefore finitely many sections
$s_i\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{X},\sh{F}(n))$ whose images generate
$\sh{F}_0(n)$ \sref[0]{0.5.2.3}.  Since $\mathfrak{J}$ is
contained in the maximal ideal of the local ring at every point
of $\mathfrak{X}$, Nakayama's lemma applied to those local rings
shows that the $s_i$ generate $\sh{F}(n)$
\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.5.1.1}.
\end{proof}

\begin{env}[5.2.5]
\label{III.5.2.5}
\textbf{Proof of the existence theorem: the projective case.}
With the notation of \sref{III.5.1.4}, suppose that $f$ is
projective, so that there is an ample $\sh{O}_X$-module $\sh{L}$
\sref[I]{I.5.5.4}.  By definition, $X_n=\widehat{X}\times_{\widehat{Y}}Y_n$
is the closed subprescheme
$X\times_Y Y_n=f^{-1}(Y_n)$ of $X$.  If $\sh{L}'=\sh{L}\otimes_{\sh{O}_X}\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}$
is the completion of $\sh{L}$, then
$\sh{L}'_0=\sh{L}/\mathfrak{J}\sh{L}$, regarded as an
$\sh{O}_{X_0}$-module; this module is ample
\sref[II]{II.4.6.13}[(i~\emph{bis})].

We may therefore apply \sref{III.5.2.4} to $\sh{L}'$ and to any
coherent $\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}$-module $\sh{F}$.  It follows that
$\sh{F}$ is isomorphic to a quotient of $\sh{G}=
\bigl(\sh{L}'^{\otimes(-n)}\bigr)^k$
for suitable integers $n>0$ and $k>0$.  The module $\sh{G}$ is the
completion of $(\sh{L}^{\otimes(-n)})^k$
\sref[I]{I.10.8.10}, and is therefore algebraizable.  Let
$u:\sh{G}\to\sh{F}$ be the canonical surjection, and put
$\sh{H}=\Ker(u)$; this is a coherent
$\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}$-module \sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.5.3.4}.  Applying the same
argument to $\sh{H}$, we find an algebraizable
$\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}$-module $\sh{K}$ and a homomorphism
$v:\sh{K}\to\sh{G}$ such that $\sh{H}=\Im(v)$.  Thus
$\sh{F}=\Coker(v)$, and $\sh{F}$ is algebraizable by
\sref{III.5.2.2}.
\end{env}

\begin{env}[5.2.6]
\label{III.5.2.6}
\textbf{Proof of the existence theorem: the quasi-projective case.}
Retain the notation of \sref{III.5.1.4}, and now suppose that $f$
is quasi-projective.  There is a projective morphism
$g:Z\to Y$ such that $X$ is identified with the $Y$-prescheme
induced on an open subset of $Z$ \sref[II]{II.5.3.2}.  If
$Z'=g^{-1}(Y')$, then $X'=X\cap Z'$.  Consequently the completion
$\widehat{X}=X_{/X'}$ is identified with the formal prescheme
induced by $\widehat{Z}=Z_{/Z'}$ on the open subset $X\cap Z'$ of
$\widehat{Z}$ \sref[I]{I.10.8.5}.

Let $\sh{F}'$ be a coherent $\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}$-module whose
support $T'$ is proper over $\widehat{Y}$.  By definition, there
is a closed subprescheme of $X'$ with underlying space
$T'\subset X'$ such that the restriction $T'\to Y'$ of $f$ is
proper.  Hence $T'$ is proper over $Y$, and is therefore closed in
$Z'$ \sref[II]{II.5.4.10}.  It follows that $\sh{F}'$ is induced
on $\widehat{X}$ by the $\sh{O}_{\widehat{Z}}$-module $\sh{G}'$
obtained by gluing $\sh{F}'$, on the open $\widehat{X}$, with the
zero sheaf on the open $\widehat Z\setmin T'$; the two sheaves agree on
their intersection $\widehat X\setmin T'$.

The module $\sh{G}'$ is coherent.  By \sref{III.5.2.5}, there is a
coherent $\sh{O}_Z$-module $\sh{G}$ such that
$\sh{G}'=\widehat{\sh{G}}$.  Let $T$ be the support of $\sh{G}$;
then $T'=T\cap Z'$ \sref[I]{I.10.8.12}.  If $h$ is the restriction
of $g$ to the reduced closed subprescheme of $Z$ having $T$ as
its underlying space, then $T'=h^{-1}(Y')=T\cap g^{-1}(Y')$.
Thus $X\cap T$ is an open subset of $T$ containing $T'$.
----- English, ega3/ega3-6.tex, page III 153 -----
\oldpage[III]{153}

\[
  ''\sh E_{pq}^2
  =
  \bigoplus_{q_1+q_2+\ldots+q_m=q}
  \shTor_p^S
  \bigl(
    \sh H_{q_1}(\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}),\ldots,
    \sh H_{q_m}(\sh P_\bullet^{(m)})
  \bigr).
  \tag{6.5.15.1}\label{III.6.5.15.1}
\]
In \sref{III.6.8} we shall study the associativity spectral
sequences to which these hypertor functors of an arbitrary
finite number of complexes give rise.
\end{env}

\subsection{Global hypertor functors of complexes of
quasi-coherent modules and K\"unneth spectral sequences: the
affine-base case}
\label{subsection:III.6.6}

\begin{env}[6.6.1]
\label{III.6.6.1}
Let $S=\Spec(A)$ be an affine scheme, and let $X^{(i)}$
$(i=1,2)$ be two quasi-compact $S$-schemes.  For $i=1,2$, let
$\sh P_\bullet^{(i)}$ be a bounded-below complex of
quasi-coherent $\sh O_{X^{(i)}}$-modules whose differential has
degree $-1$.  Choose a finite covering $\mathfrak U^{(i)}=(U_\alpha^{(i)})$
of $X^{(i)}$ by affine open subsets.  Put $X=X^{(1)}\times_S X^{(2)}$.
This is a quasi-compact $S$-scheme
(\sref[I]{I.5.5.1} and \sref[I]{I.6.6.4}), and
$\mathfrak U=\mathfrak U^{(1)}\times_S\mathfrak U^{(2)}$ is the
covering of $X$ by the affine open subsets
$U_\alpha^{(1)}\times_SU_\beta^{(2)}$.

For $p\leq0$ and $q\in\bb Z$, the group of $(-p)$-alternating
cochains $C^{-p}(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_q^{(i)})$
of the covering $\mathfrak U^{(i)}$ with coefficients in
$\sh P_q^{(i)}$ (\emph{G}, II, 5.1) is an $A$-module.  For
$p>0$, put
$C^p(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_q^{(i)})=0$.  This defines a
bicomplex of $A$-modules $C^\bullet(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)})$
whose two differentials have degree $-1$.

It follows from the definitions \sref[0]{0.12.1.2} that the
homology module $H_n\bigl(C^\bullet
(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)})\bigr)$
of this bicomplex, viewed in the usual way as a simple complex
for the total degree, is the hypercohomology module $\HH^{-n}
\bigl(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P^{(i)\bullet}\bigr)$,
where $\sh P^{(i)\bullet}$ is the complex with differential of
degree $+1$ whose component of degree $q$ is
$\sh P_{-q}^{(i)}$.  By abuse of notation we write this as $\HH^{-n}
\bigl(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)}\bigr)$.
By \sref{III.6.2.2}, this module is canonically isomorphic to $\HH^{-n}\bigl(X^{(i)},\sh P^{(i)\bullet}\bigr)$,
also written
$\HH^{-n}(X^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)})$.  It is therefore
independent of the chosen finite covering
$\mathfrak U^{(i)}$.
\end{env}

\begin{env}[6.6.2]
\label{III.6.6.2}
Apply the general theory of hyperhomology of functors with
respect to bicomplexes (\textbf{0},~11.7.4) to the two
bicomplexes of $A$-modules
\[
  L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}
  =
  C^\bullet(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)})
  \qquad(i=1,2)
\]
and to the covariant bifunctor
$L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}\otimes_A
 L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}$.
Since the cochains considered are \emph{alternating} and the coverings
$\mathfrak U^{(i)}$ are finite, note that the modules $C^{-p}(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_q^{(i)})$
are $\neq0$ only for a \emph{finite number (independent of $q$)} of values of $p$,
and in particular the \emph{two} degrees of each
$L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}$ are \emph{bounded below}.

Denote by $\mathbf{Tor}_n^A(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)},L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)})$
or by $\mathbf{Tor}_n^S
(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})$
the $n$-th hyperhomology module of
$L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}\otimes_A
 L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}$, which we shall call the \emph{hypertor} of index
$n$ of $\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}$ and $\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}$ relative
to the coverings $\mathfrak U^{(1)}$ and
$\mathfrak U^{(2)}$.  When $\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}$ and $\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}$ are reduced to
their terms of degree $0$, $\sh F^{(1)}$ and $\sh F^{(2)}$, write $\shTor_n^S
(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
\sh F^{(1)},\sh F^{(2)})$
for their hypertor.  Following the general conventions, the
same notation $\shTor_n^S
(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})$
also denotes the \emph{bicomplex} whose component of indices $(j,k)$
is $\shTor_n^S
(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
\sh P_j^{(1)},\sh P_k^{(2)})$.

Since $L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}$ is an exact functor in
$\sh P_\bullet^{(i)}$, the intersections of the sets
----- English, ega3/ega3-5.tex, page III 154 -----
\oldpage[III]{154}
Since $h$ is proper \sref[II]{II.5.4.2}, and hence closed,
\sref{III.5.1.3.1} gives $X\cap T=T$.  Thus $T\subset X$; and
since $T$ is closed in $Z$, it is proper over $Y$.  If $\sh{F}$ is
the sheaf induced on $X$ by $\sh{G}$, its completion
$\widehat{\sh{F}}$ is induced on $\widehat{X}$ by
$\widehat{\sh{G}}$ \sref[I]{I.10.8.4}, and is therefore equal to
$\sh{F}'$.  This completes the proof.
\end{env}


\subsection{Proof of the existence theorem: the general case}
\label{subsection:III.5.3}

\begin{lemma}[5.3.1]
\label{III.5.3.1}
Under the conditions of \sref{III.5.1.4}, let $0\longrightarrow\sh{H}\longrightarrow\sh{F}
\longrightarrow\sh{G}\longrightarrow0$
be an exact sequence of coherent
$\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}$-modules.  If $\sh{G}$ and $\sh{H}$ are
algebraizable, then $\sh{F}$ is algebraizable.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Suppose that
\[
  \sh{G}=\widehat{\sh{B}},
  \qquad
  \sh{H}=\widehat{\sh{C}},
\]
where $\sh{B}$ and $\sh{C}$ are coherent $\sh{O}_X$-modules whose
supports are proper over $Y$.  The module $\sh{F}$ canonically
defines an element of the $A$-module $\Ext^1_{\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}}
(\widehat{X};\widehat{\sh{B}},\widehat{\sh{C}})$
\sref[0]{0.12.3.2}.  The hypotheses imply that this $A$-module is
canonically isomorphic to $\Ext^1_{\sh{O}_X}(X;\sh{B},\sh{C})$
\sref{III.4.5.2}.  There is therefore an exact sequence $0\longrightarrow\sh{C}\longrightarrow\sh{A}
\longrightarrow\sh{B}\longrightarrow0$
of coherent $\sh{O}_X$-modules whose canonical class maps to the
class corresponding to $\sh{F}$.  By definition, taking account of
\sref[I]{I.10.8.8}, (ii), this says that $\sh{F}$ is isomorphic to
$\widehat{\sh{A}}$.  The lemma follows, since
$\Supp(\sh{A})$ is contained in
$\Supp(\sh{B})\cup\Supp(\sh{C})$ and is therefore proper over $Y$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[5.3.2]
\label{III.5.3.2}
Under the conditions of \sref{III.5.1.1}, let
$u:\sh{F}\to\sh{G}$ be a homomorphism of coherent
$\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}$-modules.  If $\sh{G}$, $\Ker(u)$, and
$\Coker(u)$ are algebraizable, then $\sh{F}$ is algebraizable.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Lemma \sref{III.5.2.2}, applied to
$\sh{G}\to\Coker(u)$, shows that $\Im(u)$ is algebraizable.  It then
suffices to apply Lemma \sref{III.5.3.1} to the exact sequence $0\longrightarrow\Ker(u)\longrightarrow\sh{F}
\longrightarrow\Im(u)\longrightarrow0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[5.3.3]
\label{III.5.3.3}
Under the conditions of \sref{III.5.1.1}, let $h:Z\to Y$ be a
morphism of finite type, let $\widehat{Z}$ be the completion of $Z$
along $Z'=h^{-1}(Y')$, let $g:Z\to X$ be a proper $Y$-morphism,
and let $\widehat{g}:\widehat{Z}\to\widehat{X}$ be its extension to
the completions.  For every algebraizable
$\sh{O}_{\widehat{Z}}$-module $\sh{F}'$, the module
$\widehat{g}_*(\sh{F}')$ is an algebraizable
$\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}$-module.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $\sh{F}$ is a coherent $\sh{O}_Z$-module such that
$\sh{F}'=\widehat{\sh{F}}$, the first comparison theorem
\sref{III.4.1.5} identifies
$\widehat{g}_*(\widehat{\sh{F}})$ with the completion of
$g_*(\sh{F})$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[5.3.4]
\label{III.5.3.4}
Let $X$ be an ordinary Noetherian scheme, let $X'$ be a closed
subset of $X$, let $f:Z\to X$ be a proper morphism, and put
\[
  Z'=f^{-1}(X'),\qquad
  \widehat{X}=X_{/X'},\qquad
  \widehat{Z}=Z_{/Z'}.
\]
Let $\widehat{f}:\widehat{Z}\to\widehat{X}$ be the extension of $f$
to the completions.  Let $\sh{M}$ be a coherent ideal of
$\sh{O}_X$ such that, if $U=X\setmin\Supp(\sh{O}_X/\sh{M})$,
the restriction $f^{-1}(U)\to U$ of $f$ is an isomorphism.  Then,
for every coherent $\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}$-module $\sh{F}$, there is
an integer $n>0$ such that the kernel and cokernel of the canonical
homomorphism $\rho_{\sh{F}}:
\sh{F}\longrightarrow
\widehat{f}_*\bigl(\widehat{f}^{\,*}(\sh{F})\bigr)$
\sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.4.4.3} are annihilated by
$\widehat{\sh{M}}^{\,n}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We may restrict to the case $X=\Spec(B)$, where $B$ is a
Noetherian ring; then $X'=\mathrm{V}(\mathfrak{K})$ for an ideal
$\mathfrak{K}$ of $B$.  We first reduce to the case in which $B$ is
\emph{adic} Noetherian and $\mathfrak{K}$ is an ideal of definition.

Let $B_1$ be the separated completion of $B$ for the
$\mathfrak{K}$-preadic topology.
----- English, ega3/ega3-6.tex, page III 154 -----
\oldpage[III]{154}
of $\mathfrak U^{(i)}$ being affine
\sref[I]{I.5.5.6} and \sref[I]{I.1.3.11}, $\mathbf{Tor}_\bullet^S
(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})$
is a \emph{covariant bi-$\partial$-functor} in
$\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}$, $\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}$, with values in
the category of $A$-modules \sref[0]{0.11.7.3}.  Moreover, we know
\sref[0]{0.11.7.2} that this bifunctor is the common abutment of six \emph{biregular}
spectral functors, which we shall denote ${}^{(t)}E^S
(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})$,
or simply ${}^{(t)}E
(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})$,
where $t$ is to be replaced by one of the letters $a$, $b$, $a'$, $b'$, $c$,
$d$, and whose $E_2$ terms are the following:
\[
\begin{aligned}
  {}^{(a)}E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \bigoplus_{q_1+q_2=q}
  \Tor_p^A
  \bigl(\mathrm H_{q_1}(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}),
        \mathrm H_{q_2}(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)})\bigr)\\
  {}^{(b)}E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \mathrm H_p\bigl(
    \mathbf{Tor}_q^{A,\mathrm{II}}
    (L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)},L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)})
  \bigr)\\
  {}^{(a')}E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \bigoplus_{q_1+q_2=q}
  \mathbf{Tor}_p^A
  \bigl(\mathrm H_{q_1}^{\mathrm I}(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}),
        \mathrm H_{q_2}^{\mathrm I}(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)})\bigr)\\
  {}^{(b')}E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \mathrm H_p\bigl(
    \Tor_q^A
    (L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)},L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)})
  \bigr)\\
  {}^{(c)}E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \bigoplus_{q_1+q_2=q}
  \mathbf{Tor}_p^A
  \bigl(\mathrm H_{q_1}^{\mathrm{II}}(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}),
        \mathrm H_{q_2}^{\mathrm{II}}(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)})\bigr)\\
  {}^{(d)}E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \mathrm H_p\bigl(
    \mathbf{Tor}_q^{A,\mathrm I}
    (L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)},L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)})
  \bigr),
\end{aligned}
\]
where the notation agrees with that of the general theory of
hyperhomology.  In what follows we shall make these initial terms more explicit.
\end{env}

\begin{env}[6.6.3]
\label{III.6.6.3}
\emph{Spectral sequences $(a)$ and $(a')$.}
We saw in \sref{III.6.6.1} that the homology module
$\mathrm H_n(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)})$ of the bicomplex
$L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}$ was equal to
$\mathbf{H}^{-n}(X^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)})$; hence
\[
  {}^{(a)}E_{pq}^2
  =
  \bigoplus_{q_1+q_2=q}
  \Tor_p^A
  \bigl(
    \mathbf{H}^{-q_1}(X^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}),
    \mathbf{H}^{-q_2}(X^{(2)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr).
\]
By definition, the complex
$\mathrm H_n^{\mathrm I}(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)})$ has as its term of degree $k$
the homology module
$\mathrm H_n(C^\bullet(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_k^{(i)}))$, that is,
by definition, the \emph{cohomology} module
$\HH^{-n}(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_k^{(i)})$; we know \sref{III.1.4.1} that
this module is canonically isomorphic to
$\HH^{-n}(X^{(i)},\sh P_k^{(i)})$; hence
\[
  {}^{(a')}E_{pq}^2
  =
  \bigoplus_{q_1+q_2=q}
  \mathbf{Tor}_p^A
  \bigl(
    \HH^{-q_1}(X^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}),
    \HH^{-q_2}(X^{(2)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr).
\]
\end{env}

\begin{env}[6.6.4]
\label{III.6.6.4}
\emph{Spectral sequences $(b)$ and $(b')$.}
By definition, $\mathbf{Tor}_q^{A,\mathrm{II}}
(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)},L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)})$
is a bicomplex whose term of degree $(h,k)$ is the $A$-module
\[
  \mathbf{Tor}_q^A
  \bigl(
    C^{-h}(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}),
    C^{-k}(\mathfrak U^{(2)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr).
\]
Let $\Phi^{(i)}$ be the index set of $\mathfrak U^{(i)}$; by
definition, the complex of modules
$C^r(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)})$ ($r\geqslant0$) is the direct sum
of the complexes $\Gamma(U_\rho^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)})$,
where $U_\rho^{(i)}$ is the intersection of the
$U_\xi^{(i)}$ for $\xi\in\rho$, and $\rho$ ranges over
$\mathfrak P(\Phi^{(i)})$; hence the $A$-module
\[
  \mathbf{Tor}_q^A
  \bigl(
    C^{-h}(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}),
    C^{-k}(\mathfrak U^{(2)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr)
\]
is the direct sum of the $A$-modules $\mathbf{Tor}_q^A
\bigl(
\Gamma(U_\sigma^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}),
\Gamma(U_\tau^{(2)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
\bigr)$,
where $\sigma$ (resp. $\tau$) ranges over the elements of
$\mathfrak P(\Phi^{(1)})$ (resp. $\mathfrak P(\Phi^{(2)})$) such that
$\operatorname{Card}(\sigma)=1-h$ (resp.
$\operatorname{Card}(\tau)=1-k$).  Since $X^{(1)}$ and $X^{(2)}$ are schemes, the
$U_\rho^{(i)}$ are affine, so we have \sref{III.6.4.1.1}
\[
  \mathbf{Tor}_q^A
  \bigl(
    \Gamma(U_\sigma^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}),
    \Gamma(U_\tau^{(2)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr)
  =
  \Gamma\bigl(
    U_\sigma^{(1)}\times_SU_\tau^{(2)},
    \shTor_q^S(\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr).
\]

\end{proof}

\oldpage[III]{155}
If $\mathfrak{K}_1=\mathfrak{K}B_1$, then $B_1$ is an adic
Noetherian ring and $\mathfrak{K}_1$ is an ideal of definition.
Put $X_1=\Spec(B_1)$, and let $h:X_1\to X$ be the morphism
corresponding to the canonical homomorphism $B\to B_1$.  If
$X'_1=h^{-1}(X')$, then $X'_1=\mathrm{V}(\mathfrak{K}_1)$.  Finally
put
\[
  Z_1=Z\times_X X_1,\qquad f_1:Z_1\longrightarrow X_1.
\]
The morphism $f_1$ is proper \sref[II]{II.5.4.2}.  Denote by
$\widehat{X}_1$ the completion of $X_1$ along $X'_1$, by $\widehat{Z}_1=
Z_1\times_{X_1}\widehat{X}_1$
the completion of $Z_1$ along
$Z'_1=f_1^{-1}(X'_1)$, and by
$\widehat{f}_1$ the extension of $f_1$ to the completions.

The extension
$\widehat{h}:\widehat{X}_1\to\widehat{X}$ corresponds to the
identity map of $B_1$ and is therefore an isomorphism
\sref[I]{I.10.9.1}.  The corresponding morphism
$\widehat{Z}_1\to\widehat{Z}$ is consequently also an isomorphism,
and these isomorphisms identify $\widehat{f}_1$ with
$\widehat{f}$.  Moreover,
$\sh{M}_1=h^*(\sh{M})$ is a coherent ideal of $\sh{O}_{X_1}$ and $\Supp(\sh{O}_{X_1}/\sh{M}_1)
=h^{-1}\bigl(\Supp(\sh{O}_X/\sh{M})\bigr)$
\sref[I]{I.9.1.13}.  Thus, if
$U_1=X_1\setmin\Supp(\sh{O}_{X_1}/\sh{M}_1)$, then
$U_1=h^{-1}(U)$, and the restriction
$f_1^{-1}(U_1)\to U_1$ is an isomorphism
\sref[I]{I.3.2.7}.  Finally, $\widehat{\sh{M}}$ and
$\widehat{\sh{M}}_1$ are identified by $\widehat{h}$
\sref[I]{I.10.9.5}.  All the hypotheses of the lemma are therefore
satisfied by $X_1$, $X'_1$, $f_1$, and $\sh{M}_1$; henceforth we
may suppose that $B$ is adic Noetherian and that $\mathfrak{K}$ is
an ideal of definition.

We then have $\widehat{X}=\Spf(B)$ and
$\sh{F}=N^\Delta$, where $N$ is a finite-type $B$-module.  Thus
$\sh{F}=\widehat{\sh{G}}$, where $\sh{G}$ is the coherent
$\sh{O}_X$-module $\widetilde{N}$ \sref[I]{I.10.10.5}, and
\[
  \widehat{f}^{\,*}(\sh{F})
  =(f^*(\sh{G}))^{\widehat{\,}}
\]
\sref[I]{I.10.9.5}.  By the first comparison theorem
\sref{III.4.1.5},
$\widehat{f}_*((f^*(\sh{G}))^{\widehat{\,}})$ is canonically identified with
$(f_*(f^*(\sh{G})))^{\widehat{\,}}$,
and, by \sref{III.5.1.3}, the canonical homomorphism
$\rho_{\sh{F}}$ is precisely $\widehat{\rho}_{\sh{G}}$.

The kernel $\sh{P}$ and cokernel $\sh{R}$ of $\rho_{\sh{G}}:
\sh{G}\longrightarrow f_*\bigl(f^*(\sh{G})\bigr)$
are coherent $\sh{O}_X$-modules, and their restrictions to $U$ are
zero.  There is therefore an integer $n>0$ such that $\sh{M}^n\sh{P}=\sh{M}^n\sh{R}=0$
\sref[I]{I.9.3.4}.  It follows that $\widehat{\sh{M}}^{\,n}\widehat{\sh{P}}
=
\widehat{\sh{M}}^{\,n}\widehat{\sh{R}}
=0$
\sref[I]{I.10.8.8} \sref[I]{I.10.8.10}, which proves the lemma.
\end{proof}

\begin{env}[5.3.5]
\label{III.5.3.5}
\textbf{End of the proof of the existence theorem.}

The hypotheses being those of \sref{III.5.1.4}, we shall use the
principle of Noetherian induction \sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.2.2.2}, thus supposing
the theorem true for every closed subprescheme $T$ of $X$ whose
underlying space is distinct from $X$ (the completion $\widehat{T}$
being of course the completion of $T$ along $T\cap X'$).  We may
suppose $X$ nonempty.  Since $f$ is separated and of finite type, we
may apply Chow's lemma \sref[II]{II.5.6.1}: there therefore exist a
$Y$-scheme $Z$ and a $Y$-morphism $g:Z\to X$ such that the structural
morphism $h:Z\to Y$ is quasi-projective, the morphism $g$ projective
and surjective, and moreover a nonempty open subset $U$ of $X$ such
that the restriction $g^{-1}(U)\to U$ is an isomorphism.  Let $\sh{M}$
be a coherent ideal of $\sh{O}_X$ defining a closed subprescheme with
underlying space $X\setmin U$ \sref[I]{I.5.2.2}, and let $\sh{F}$ be a
coherent $\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}$-module whose support $E$ is proper over
$Y$; denote by $\widehat{Z}$ the completion of $Z$ along $h^{-1}(Y')$,
and by $\widehat{g}:\widehat{Z}\to\widehat{X}$ the extension of $g$ to
the completions.  Then $\widehat{g}^{\,*}(\sh{F})$ is a coherent
$\sh{O}_{\widehat{Z}}$-module whose support is contained in
$g^{-1}(E)$ and is consequently proper over $Y$, since $g$ is
projective, hence proper \sref[II]{II.5.4.6}.  Since $h$ is
quasi-projective, $\widehat{g}^{\,*}(\sh{F})$ is algebraizable by
virtue of \sref{III.5.2.6}.
----- English, ega3/ega3-6.tex, page III 155 -----
\oldpage[III]{155}

We thus see that ${}^{(b)}E_{pq}^2$ is the $(-p)$-th cohomology
module of the complex $L^\bullet(\Phi^{(1)},\Phi^{(2)};\sh S)$
of \emph{bialternating} cochains on $\Phi^{(1)}$ and $\Phi^{(2)}$ with
values in the \emph{coefficient system}
\[
  \sh S:(\sigma,\tau)\leadsto
  \Gamma\bigl(
    U_\sigma^{(1)}\times_SU_\tau^{(2)},
    \shTor_q^S(\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr)
\]
\sref[0]{0.11.8.4}.  We then know, by \sref[0]{0.11.8.5} and
\sref[0]{0.11.8.6}, that the cohomology of this complex is the same as that of the
complex $C^\bullet(\Phi^{(1)},\Phi^{(2)};\sh S)$ of \emph{all} cochains on
$\Phi^{(1)}$ and $\Phi^{(2)}$ with values in $\sh S$, and also as that of the
complex $P^\bullet(\Phi^{(1)},\Phi^{(2)};\sh S)$,
whose elements are the linear combinations of the
\[
  \lambda(\sigma,\tau)\in
  \Gamma\bigl(
    U_\sigma^{(1)}\times_SU_\tau^{(2)},
    \shTor_q^S(\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr),
\]
where
\[
  \sigma=(\alpha_0,\ldots,\alpha_h),
  \qquad
  \tau=(\beta_0,\ldots,\beta_h)
\]
are sequences having \emph{the same number of elements}.  But then
\[
\begin{split}
  U_\sigma^{(1)}\times_SU_\tau^{(2)}
  &=
  (U_{\alpha_0}^{(1)}\times_SU_{\beta_0}^{(2)})
  \cap\ldots\cap
  (U_{\alpha_h}^{(1)}\times_SU_{\beta_h}^{(2)})
\end{split}
\]
\sref[I]{I.3.2.7}.  If we denote by $\mathfrak U$ the covering of
$Z=X^{(1)}\times_SX^{(2)}$ by the affine open subsets
$U_\alpha^{(1)}\times_SU_\beta^{(2)}$, we finally see, taking into account
that $X^{(1)}\times_SX^{(2)}$ is a \emph{scheme}, that by virtue of
\sref{III.1.3.1} we have
\[
  {}^{(b)}E_{pq}^2
  =
  \mathbf{H}^{-p}
  \bigl(
    X^{(1)}\times_SX^{(2)},\shTor_q^S
    (\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr).
\]

Secondly, $\Tor_q^A
(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)},L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)})$
is a bicomplex whose term of degree $(h,k)$ is the direct
sum of the $A$-modules
\[
  \Tor_q^A
  \bigl(
    C^{-h_1}(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\sh P_{k_1}^{(1)}),
    C^{-h_2}(\mathfrak U^{(2)},\sh P_{k_2}^{(2)})
  \bigr),
\]
where $h_1+h_2=h$ and $k_1+k_2=k$; making the modules
$C^r(\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_s^{(i)})$ explicit as above, we see again
that this term is the direct sum of the $A$-modules
\[
  \Gamma\bigl(
    U_\sigma^{(1)}\times_SU_\tau^{(2)},
    \shTor_q^S(\sh P_{k_1}^{(1)},\sh P_{k_2}^{(2)})
  \bigr)
\]
where $k_1+k_2=k$, and $\sigma$ (resp. $\tau$) ranges over the elements of
$\mathfrak P(\Phi^{(1)})$ (resp. $\mathfrak P(\Phi^{(2)})$) such that
$\operatorname{Card}(\sigma)+\operatorname{Card}(\tau)=2-h$.  The term
${}^{(b')}E_{pq}^2$ that we are computing is the $(-p)$-th cohomology module
of a bicomplex $N^{\bullet\bullet}=(N^{hk})$, where the simple complex
$N^{\bullet k}$ is the complex of \emph{bialternating}
cochains on $\Phi^{(1)}$ and $\Phi^{(2)}$, with values in the
coefficient system
\[
  \sh S_k:(\sigma,\tau)\leadsto
  \Gamma\left(
    U_\sigma^{(1)}\times_SU_\tau^{(2)},
    \bigoplus_{k_1+k_2=k}
    \shTor_q^S(\sh P_{-k_1}^{(1)},\sh P_{-k_2}^{(2)})
  \right),
\]
these coefficient systems forming a \emph{complex}
$\sh S^\bullet$ whose differential comes from that of
the simple complex associated to the bicomplex $\shTor_q^S
(\sh P^{(1)\bullet},\sh P^{(2)\bullet})$.
We know that the cohomology of $N^{\bullet\bullet}$ is the same as that of
the bicomplex $C^\bullet
(\Phi^{(1)},\Phi^{(2)};\sh S^\bullet)$
\sref[0]{0.11.8.9}, and also the same as that of the bicomplex $P^\bullet
(\Phi^{(1)},\Phi^{(2)};\sh S^\bullet)$,
whose elements of degrees $(h,k)$ are the linear combinations of the
\[
  \lambda(\sigma,\tau)\in
  \Gamma\left(
    U_\sigma^{(1)}\times_SU_\tau^{(2)},
    \bigoplus_{k_1+k_2=k}
    \shTor_q^S(\sh P_{-k_1}^{(1)},\sh P_{-k_2}^{(2)})
  \right)
\]
$\sigma=(\alpha_0,\ldots,\alpha_h)$ and
$\tau=(\beta_0,\ldots,\beta_h)$ being sequences having \emph{the same number
of elements} \sref[0]{0.11.8.10}.  We then see as above that
${}^{(b')}E_{pq}^2$ is the $(-p)$-th cohomology module of the
bicomplex $C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\sh Q^\bullet)$,
where $\sh Q^\bullet$ is the simple complex associated to
the bicomplex $\shTor_q^S
(\sh P^{(1)\bullet},\sh P^{(2)\bullet})$
of $\sh O_Z$-modules.  With the conventions made in
\sref{III.6.6.1}, we therefore have
\[
  {}^{(b')}E_{pq}^2
  =
  \mathbf{H}^{-p}
  \bigl(
    X^{(1)}\times_SX^{(2)},\shTor_q^S
    (\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr).
\]
\end{env}
----- English, ega3/ega3-5.tex, page III 156 -----
\oldpage[III]{156}
We conclude that $\widehat{g}_*\bigl(\widehat{g}^{\,*}(\sh{F})\bigr)$
is an algebraizable $\sh{O}_{\widehat{X}}$-module \sref{III.5.3.3},
since $g$ is proper.  We can now apply to $\sh{F}$ and to $g$ the
result of \sref{III.5.3.4}: the kernel $\sh{P}$ and the cokernel
$\sh{R}$ of the homomorphism $\rho_{\sh{F}}:\sh{F}\to
\widehat{g}_*\bigl(\widehat{g}^{\,*}(\sh{F})\bigr)$ are annihilated by
a power $\widehat{\sh{M}}^{\,n}$; let $T$ be the closed subprescheme
of $X$ defined by $\sh{M}^n$, having $X\setmin U$ as underlying space,
and $j:T\to X$ the canonical injection, so that the extension to the
completions $\widehat{j}:\widehat{T}\to\widehat{X}$ is the canonical
injection \sref[I]{I.10.14.7}.  We may therefore write
$\sh{P}=\widehat{j}_*\bigl(\widehat{j}^{\,*}(\sh{P})\bigr)$ and
$\sh{R}=\widehat{j}_*\bigl(\widehat{j}^{\,*}(\sh{R})\bigr)$, and since
$U$ is not empty, it follows from the induction hypothesis that
$\widehat{j}^{\,*}(\sh{P})$ and $\widehat{j}^{\,*}(\sh{R})$ are
algebraizable $\sh{O}_{\widehat{T}}$-modules; by virtue of
\sref{III.5.3.3}, $\sh{P}$ and $\sh{R}$ are algebraizable, and we can
then apply \sref{III.5.3.2}, which finally proves that $\sh{F}$ is
algebraizable.

\hfill Q.E.D.
\end{env}


\subsection{Application: comparison between morphisms of ordinary schemes and morphisms of formal schemes. Algebraizable formal schemes}
\label{subsection:III.5.4}

\begin{theorem}[5.4.1]
\label{III.5.4.1}
Let $A$ be an adic Noetherian ring, let $\mathfrak{J}$ be an ideal
of definition of $A$, and put
\[
  S=\Spec(A),\qquad S'=\mathrm{V}(\mathfrak{J}).
\]
Let $u:X\to S$ be a proper morphism and let $v:Y\to S$ be a
separated morphism of finite type.  Let
$\widehat{S}$, $\widehat{X}$, and $\widehat{Y}$ be the completions
of $S$, $X$, and $Y$ along $S'$, $u^{-1}(S')$, and $v^{-1}(S')$,
respectively.  For every $S$-morphism $f:X\to Y$, let
$\widehat{f}:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{Y}$ be its extension to the
completions.  Then the map $f\leadsto\widehat{f}$ is a bijection
\[
  \Hom_S(X,Y)
  \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }
  \Hom_{\widehat{S}}(\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We first show that $f\leadsto\widehat{f}$ is injective.  Suppose indeed that $f,g:X\to Y$ are
two $S$-morphisms such that $\widehat{f}=\widehat{g}$.  There is then
an open neighbourhood $V$ of $X'=u^{-1}(S')$ on which $f$ and $g$
coincide \sref[I]{I.10.9.4}.  Since $u$ is closed,
\sref{III.5.1.3.1} gives $V=X$, and hence $f=g$.

We now prove that $f\leadsto\widehat{f}$ is surjective; so let
$h:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{Y}$ be an
$\widehat{S}$-morphism.  Put $Z=X\times_S Y$,
and denote the canonical projections by $p:Z\to X$ and
$q:Z\to Y$.  The prescheme $Z$ is of finite type over $S$
\sref[I]{I.6.3.4}, and is therefore Noetherian.  Let
$\widehat{Z}$ be its completion along $Z'=p^{-1}\bigl(u^{-1}(S')\bigr)$.
The formal prescheme $\widehat{Z}$ is canonically identified with
$\widehat{X}\times_{\widehat{S}}\widehat{Y}$, and its projections
onto $\widehat{X}$ and $\widehat{Y}$ are identified with
$\widehat{p}$ and $\widehat{q}$ \sref[I]{I.10.9.7}.

Since $Y$ is separated over $S$, $\widehat{Y}$ is separated over
$\widehat{S}$ \sref[I]{I.10.15.7}.  The graph morphism $\Gamma_h=(1_{\widehat{X}},h):
\widehat{X}\longrightarrow\widehat{Z}$
is consequently a closed immersion \sref[I]{I.10.15.4}.  Let
$\mathfrak{T}$ be the closed formal subprescheme of
$\widehat{Z}$ associated with this immersion, and let
$j:\mathfrak{T}\to\widehat{Z}$ be the canonical injection.  Thus $\Gamma_h=j\circ w$,
where $w:\widehat{X}\to\mathfrak{T}$ is an isomorphism
\sref[I]{I.10.14.3} whose inverse is
$\widehat{p}\circ j$.  Moreover, $\mathfrak{T}$ is obviously proper over
$\widehat{S}$, since $\widehat{X}$ is.  It follows from
\sref{III.5.1.8} that there is a closed subprescheme $T$ of $Z$
such that $\mathfrak{T}
=\widehat{T}
=T_{/(T\cap Z')}$,
and that $j=\widehat{i}$, where $i:T\to Z$ is the canonical
injection \sref[I]{I.10.14.7}.

Then $p\circ i:T\to X$ is an isomorphism, since this is so for
$(p\circ i)^{\widehat{\,}}=\widehat{p}\circ\widehat{i}$ by hypothesis,
and it suffices to apply
\sref{III.4.6.8}, noting as above that $S$ is the only
neighbourhood of $S'$ in $S$.
----- English, ega3/ega3-6.tex, page III 156 -----
\oldpage[III]{156}

\begin{env}[6.6.5]
\label{III.6.6.5}
\emph{Spectral sequences \textnormal{(c)} and
\textnormal{(d)}.}
By definition, the component of degree $h$ of the complex
$H_n^{\mathrm{II}}(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)})$ is the
$A$-module $C^{-h}
\bigl(
\mathfrak U^{(i)},
\sh H_n(\sh P_\bullet^{(i)})
\bigr)$,
because the functor $C^{-h}$ is exact.  Hence, by the definition
of the hypertor of two modules relative to two coverings
\sref{III.6.6.2},
\[
  {}^{(c)}E_{pq}^2
  =
  \bigoplus_{q_1+q_2=q}
  \operatorname{Tor}_p^S
  \bigl(
    \mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
    \sh H_{q_1}(\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}),
    \sh H_{q_2}(\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr).
\]

Finally, by definition, $\mathbf{Tor}_q^{A,\mathrm I}
(L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)},L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)})$
is a bicomplex whose component of bidegree $(h,k)$ is the
$A$-module $\operatorname{Tor}_q^S
\bigl(
\mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
\sh P_h^{(1)},\sh P_k^{(2)}
\bigr)$.
Consequently
\[
  {}^{(d)}E_{pq}^2
  =
  H_p\left(
    \operatorname{Tor}_q^S
    \bigl(
      \mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
      \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
    \bigr)
  \right).
\]
\end{env}

\begin{env}[6.6.6]
\label{III.6.6.6}
The theory of hyperhomology for functors of bicomplexes
\sref[0]{0.11.7.3} shows, as in \sref{III.6.3.4}, that for
every \emph{flat} Cartan--Eilenberg resolution
$M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}$ of $L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}$ in the
category of complexes of modules bounded below $(i=1,2)$, there
are canonical isomorphisms of bi-$\partial$-functors
\begin{env}[6.6.6.1]
\label{III.6.6.6.1}
\[
\begin{split}
  \mathbf{Tor}_\bullet^S
  \bigl(
    \mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  &\xrightarrow{\sim}
  H_\bullet
  \bigl(
    M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}
    \otimes_A M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}
  \bigr)\\
  &\xrightarrow{\sim}
  H_\bullet
  \bigl(
    M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}
    \otimes_A L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}
  \bigr)\\
  &\xrightarrow{\sim}
  H_\bullet
  \bigl(
    L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}
    \otimes_A M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}
  \bigr).
\end{split}
\tag{6.6.6.1}
\]
\end{env}
\end{env}

\begin{env}[6.6.7]
\label{III.6.6.7}
We shall now show that the global hypertor defined in
\sref{III.6.6.2}, and the six corresponding spectral sequences,
do not depend, up to canonical isomorphisms, on the finite affine
open coverings $\mathfrak U^{(i)}$ used to define them.  It is
enough to show that if $\mathfrak V^{(i)}$ are two other
coverings of the same kind, with $\mathfrak V^{(i)}$ finer than
$\mathfrak U^{(i)}$ for $i=1,2$, then there are canonical
isomorphisms of spectral functors
\begin{env}[6.6.7, $t$]
\label{III.6.6.7.t}
\[
  {}^{(t)}E
  \bigl(
    \mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \xrightarrow{\sim}
  {}^{(t)}E
  \bigl(
    \mathfrak V^{(1)},\mathfrak V^{(2)};
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr),
  \tag{6.6.7, $t$)}\label{III.6.6.7.t.eq}
\]
\end{env}
where $t$ is one of $a,b,a',b',c,d$.

For $i=1,2$ there are homomorphisms of bicomplexes
\[
  C^\bullet
  (\mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)})
  \longrightarrow
  C^\bullet
  (\mathfrak V^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)})
\]
that are well defined up to homotopy
((G,~II, 5.7.1)).  It follows at once that the
homomorphisms \eqref{III.6.6.7.t.eq} are canonically defined and
compatible with the boundary operators in their abutments
\sref[0]{0.11.3.2}.  Moreover, the calculation of the
$E_2$-terms of the spectral sequences
\textnormal{(a)}, \textnormal{(b)}, \textnormal{(a$'$)}, and
\textnormal{(b$'$)} shows that
\eqref{III.6.6.7.t.eq} is an isomorphism on their $E_2$-terms.
Since these spectral sequences are biregular, it follows that
\eqref{III.6.6.7.t.eq} is an isomorphism for these spectral
functors, and hence an isomorphism of bi-$\partial$-functors on
their common abutment \sref[0]{0.11.1.5}.

In particular, if $\sh F^{(i)}$ is a quasi-coherent
$\sh O_{X^{(i)}}$-module $(i=1,2)$, the canonical homomorphism
\[
  \operatorname{Tor}_\bullet^S
  \bigl(
    \mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
    \sh F^{(1)},\sh F^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \longrightarrow
  \operatorname{Tor}_\bullet^S
  \bigl(
    \mathfrak V^{(1)},\mathfrak V^{(2)};
    \sh F^{(1)},\sh F^{(2)}
  \bigr)
\]
is bijective.  In view of the calculation in
\sref{III.6.6.5}, the homomorphism
\eqref{III.6.6.7.t.eq} is therefore also an isomorphism on
$E_2$-terms for $t=c$ and $t=d$.  As above, we conclude that it
is an isomorphism of spectral sequences for these two values of
$t$.

différentielle provient de celle du complexe simple associé au bicomplexe
$\mathscr{T}\!or_q^S
(\mathscr P^{(1)\bullet},\mathscr P^{(2)\bullet})$. On sait que la
cohomologie de $N^{\bullet\bullet}$ est la même que celle du bicomplex

d'éléments

d'éléments}
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): \tau)\in
  \Gamma\left(U_\sigma^{(1)}\times_S U_\tau^{(2)},
    \bigoplus_{k_1+k_2=k}

$\sigma=(\alpha_0,\ldots,\alpha_h)$,

bicomplexe $C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathscr Q^\bullet)$, où

----- English, ega3/ega3-5.tex, page III 157 -----

Let $g:X\to T$ be the inverse of $p\circ i$, and put $f=q\circ i\circ g$,
which is a morphism $X\to Y$ whose graph is by definition $\Gamma_f=i\circ g$.  Since
$\widehat{g}$ is the inverse of
$(p\circ i)^{\widehat{\,}}=w^{-1}$, we have $(\Gamma_f)^{\widehat{\,}}
=\widehat{i}\circ\widehat{g}
=j\circ w
=\Gamma_h$.
But $(\Gamma_f)^{\widehat{\,}}=\Gamma_{\widehat{f}}$
\sref[I]{I.10.9.8}; hence $h=\widehat{f}$, which completes the
proof.
\end{proof}

We can therefore say, in the language of categories, that the functor
$X\leadsto\widehat{X}$ is \emph{fully faithful}
\sref[0]{0.8.1.6} from the category of schemes proper over
$\Spec(A)$ to the category of formal schemes proper over
$\Spf(A)$, for every adic Noetherian ring $A$; it consequently
establishes an equivalence between the first of these categories and a
\emph{subcategory} of the second; the objects of the latter will
be called \emph{algebraizable formal schemes}.  For
such a scheme $\mathfrak{X}$, there exists an ordinary
scheme $X$, proper over $\Spec(A)$, determined up to unique
isomorphism, such that $\mathfrak{X}$ is isomorphic to $\widehat{X}$.

\begin{scholium}[5.4.2]
\label{III.5.4.2}
With the notation of \sref{III.5.4.1}, put
\[
  S_n=\Spec(A/\mathfrak{J}^{n+1}),\qquad
  X_n=X\times_S S_n,\qquad
  Y_n=Y\times_S S_n.
\]
It follows from \sref{III.5.4.1} and \sref[I]{I.10.12.3} that
giving an $S$-morphism $f:X\to Y$ is equivalent to giving an adic
inductive $(S_n)$-system \sref[I]{I.10.12.2} of
$S_n$-morphisms $f_n:X_n\to Y_n$.
\end{scholium}

\begin{remark}[5.4.3]
\label{III.5.4.3}
Contrary to what the existence theorem \sref{III.5.1.6} might
suggest, there are formal schemes proper over $\Spf(A)$ which
are \emph{not algebraizable} (just as there are compact analytic
spaces which do not arise from complex algebraic varieties).  We
shall encounter such schemes later in the ``theory of moduli,''
which deals precisely (when the base field is $\mathbb{C}$) with
infinitesimal variations of the complex structure of a complete
algebraic variety, and it is well known that such variations can give
rise to analytic varieties which are not algebraic.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[5.4.4]
\label{III.5.4.4}
Let $A$ be an adic Noetherian ring, put
$\mathfrak{S}=\Spf(A)$, and let
\[
  g:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{S},
  \qquad
  h:\mathfrak{Y}\to\mathfrak{S}
\]
be two proper morphisms of formal schemes.  Let
$f:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}$ be an
$\mathfrak{S}$-morphism.  If $f$ is finite and
$\mathfrak{Y}$ is algebraizable, then $\mathfrak{X}$ is
algebraizable.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Note that the hypotheses on $g$ and $h$ already imply that $f$ is proper
\sref{III.3.4.1}, and that for $f$ to be finite, it is enough that, for every
$y\in\mathfrak{Y}$, the fibre $f^{-1}(y)$ be \emph{finite}
\sref{III.4.8.11}.  The hypothesis implies that $\sh{B}=f_*(\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{X}})$
is a coherent $\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}$-algebra
\sref{III.4.8.6}; hence it follows from the existence theorem that, if
$\mathfrak{Y}=\widehat{Y}$ and $h=\widehat{w}$, where
$w:Y\to\Spec(A)$ is a proper morphism of ordinary schemes, there
exists a coherent $\sh{O}_Y$-algebra $\sh{C}$
such that $\sh{B}=\widehat{\sh{C}}$.  Let $X=\Spec(\sh{C})$,
and let $u:X\to Y$ be the structural morphism; then it follows
immediately from the definition of
$\mathfrak{X}$ in terms of $\sh{B}$ \sref{III.4.8.7} that
$\mathfrak{X}$ is canonically isomorphic to $\widehat{X}$ and
that $f$ is identified with $\widehat{u}$ (to see this, it suffices
to consider the case where $Y$ is affine).

Note that \sref{III.5.1.8} is a special case of
\sref{III.5.4.4}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[5.4.5]
\label{III.5.4.5}
Let $A$ be an adic Noetherian ring, let $\mathfrak{J}$ be an ideal
of definition of $A$, put
\[
  S=\Spec(A),\qquad
  \mathfrak{S}=\widehat{S}=\Spf(A),
\]
and let $f:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{S}$ be a proper morphism of
formal schemes.  Put
\[
  S_k=\Spec(A/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}),\qquad
  X_k=\mathfrak{X}\times_{\mathfrak{S}}S_k,
\]
and, for every $\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}$-module $\sh{F}$, put $\sh{F}_k
=\sh{F}\otimes_{\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}}\sh{O}_{X_k}
=\sh{F}/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}\sh{F}$.

\oldpage[III]{157}

The isomorphisms \eqref{III.6.6.7.t.eq} may be regarded as
defining inductive systems of spectral functors on the filtering
set of pairs
$(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)})$ of finite affine open
coverings of $X^{(1)}$ and $X^{(2)}$.  We denote the inductive
limit by
\[
  {}^{(t)}E
  \bigl(
    X^{(1)},X^{(2)};
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \quad\text{or}\quad
  {}^{(t)}E^S
  \bigl(
    X^{(1)},X^{(2)};
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr).
\]
Its abutment is denoted $\mathbf{Tor}_\bullet^S
\bigl(
X^{(1)},X^{(2)};
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
\bigr)$
and is called the \emph{global hypertor} of the two complexes
$\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}$ and $\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}$.  If the two
complexes are concentrated in degree zero, with terms
$\sh F^{(1)}$ and $\sh F^{(2)}$, we write
\[
  \operatorname{Tor}_\bullet^S
  \bigl(
    X^{(1)},X^{(2)};
    \sh F^{(1)},\sh F^{(2)}
  \bigr).
\]
In accordance with the general conventions, $\operatorname{Tor}_q^S
\bigl(
X^{(1)},X^{(2)};
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
\bigr)$
will denote the bicomplex whose component of bidegree $(h,k)$ is $\operatorname{Tor}_q^S
\bigl(
X^{(1)},X^{(2)};
\sh P_h^{(1)},\sh P_k^{(2)}
\bigr)$.
\end{env}

\begin{env}[6.6.8]
\label{III.6.6.8}
Under the hypotheses of \sref{III.6.6.1}, consider two
$S$-morphisms $f_i:X^{(i)}\longrightarrow Y^{(i)}$,
where
$Y^{(i)}=\Spec(B_i)$ is an \emph{affine} $S$-\emph{scheme}, so that $B_i$ is
an $A$-algebra $(i=1,2)$.  This defines an $A$-homomorphism $B_i\longrightarrow
\Gamma(X^{(i)},\sh O_{X^{(i)}})$
\sref[I]{I.2.2.4}, and consequently each
$L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}$ defined in \sref{III.6.6.2} is a
bicomplex of $B_i$-modules.  Thus $L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}
\otimes_A L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}$
is a quadricomplex of $(B_1\otimes_AB_2)$-modules, and its six
hyperhomology spectral functors may be regarded as taking values
in the category of spectral sequences of
$(B_1\otimes_AB_2)$-\emph{modules}.

Put $Y=Y^{(1)}\times_SY^{(2)}
=\Spec(B_1\otimes_AB_2)$.
We may then consider the quasi-coherent $\sh O_Y$-modules
associated with these modules \sref[I]{I.1.3.4}.  For
$t=a,b,a',b',c,d$, write ${}^{(t)}\sh E
\bigl(
f_1,f_2;
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
\bigr)$
for the six spectral sequences of $\sh O_Y$-modules $\left(
{}^{(t)}E
\bigl(
X^{(1)},X^{(2)};
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
\bigr)
\right)\supertilde$,
and write $\boldsymbol{\mathscr{T}\!or}_\bullet^S
\bigl(
f_1,f_2;
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
\bigr)$
for their common abutment $\left(
\mathbf{Tor}_\bullet^S
\bigl(
X^{(1)},X^{(2)};
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
\bigr)
\right)\supertilde$.
When $\sh P_\bullet^{(i)}$ is concentrated in degree zero, with
term $\sh F^{(i)}$ $(i=1,2)$, we write this abutment as $\mathscr{T}\!or_\bullet^S
\bigl(
f_1,f_2;
\sh F^{(1)},\sh F^{(2)}
\bigr)$.
\end{env}


\subsection{Global hypertor functors of complexes of quasi-coherent modules and K\"unneth spectral sequences: the general case}
\label{subsection:III.6.7}

\begin{env}[6.7.1]
\label{III.6.7.1}
We now generalize the definitions of \sref{III.6.6.8} to the
case where $S$ is an arbitrary prescheme, $X^{(i)}$ and
$Y^{(i)}$ are $S$-preschemes, and $f_i:X^{(i)}\longrightarrow Y^{(i)}$
are separated and quasi-compact $S$-morphisms.  For every pair
of bounded-below complexes $\sh P_\bullet^{(i)}$ of
quasi-coherent $\sh O_{X^{(i)}}$-modules $(i=1,2)$, the aim is to
define a quasi-coherent $\sh O_Y$-module $\boldsymbol{\mathscr{T}\!or}_n^S
\bigl(
f_1,f_2;
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
\bigr)$
for every $n$, together with six spectral functors, in such a way
that these definitions reduce to those of \sref{III.6.6.8} when
$S$, $Y^{(1)}$, and $Y^{(2)}$ are affine; here
$Y=Y^{(1)}\times_SY^{(2)}$.

First suppose that $S=\Spec(A)$ is affine, while $Y^{(1)}$ and
$Y^{(2)}$ are arbitrary.  Let $W^{(i)}$ be an affine open subset
of $Y^{(i)}$.  Then $f_i^{-1}(W^{(i)})$ is a quasi-compact
$S$-scheme, and $W=W^{(1)}\times_SW^{(2)}$
is an affine open subset of $Y$.  Let $f_i':f_i^{-1}(W^{(i)})\longrightarrow W^{(i)}$
be the restriction of $f_i$, and let
\[
  \sh P_\bullet^{\prime(i)}
  =
  \sh P_\bullet^{(i)}
  \bigm|_{f_i^{-1}(W^{(i)})}
  \qquad(i=1,2).
\]
By \sref{III.6.6.8} we then have the spectral sequences of
quasi-coherent $(\sh O_Y|W)$-modules
\[
  {}^{(t)}E
  \bigl(
    f_1',f_2';
    \sh P_\bullet^{\prime(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{\prime(2)}
  \bigr),
\]
and it remains to verify that they satisfy the gluing conditions
of \sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.3.3.1}.

We are immediately reduced to the case in which
\[
  Y^{(i)}=\Spec(B_i),
  \qquad
  W^{(i)}=D(g_i),
  \qquad g_i\in B_i,
\]
so that $W=D(g_1\otimes g_2)$
in $Y=\Spec(B_1\otimes_AB_2)$
\sref[II]{II.4.3.2.1}.  Put
$X^{\prime(i)}=f_i^{-1}(W^{(i)})$.  We must establish an
isomorphism of spectral functors
\begin{env}[6.7.1.1]
\label{III.6.7.1.1}
\[
\begin{split}
  {}^{(t)}E
  \bigl(
    X^{\prime(1)},X^{\prime(2)};
    \sh P_\bullet^{\prime(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{\prime(2)}
  \bigr)
  \xrightarrow{\sim}
  {}^{(t)}E
  \bigl(
    X^{(1)},X^{(2)};
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \otimes_BB_g ,
\end{split}
\tag{6.7.1.1}\label{III.6.7.1.1.eq}
\]
\end{env}
where
\[
  B=B_1\otimes_AB_2,
  \qquad
  g=g_1\otimes g_2.
\]

\subsection{Application : comparaison de morphismes de schémas usuels et de morphismes de schémas formels. Schémas formels algébrisables.}
\label{subsection:III.5.4-fr}

\begin{theorem}[5.4.1]
\label{III.5.4.1-fr}
Soient $A$ un anneau adique noethérien, $\mathfrak J$ un idéal de définition de
$A$, $S=\operatorname{Spec}(A)$, $S'=\mathrm V(\mathfrak J)$. Soient
$u:X\to S$ un morphisme propre, $v:

=================== PAGE III 157 ===================
----- (English page III 156: shown above in this file) -----
----- (English page III 157: shown above in this file) -----
----- English, ega3/ega3-5.tex, page III 158 -----
\oldpage[III]{158}
Let $\sh{L}$ be an invertible
$\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}$-module, and suppose that $\sh{L}_0=\sh{L}/\mathfrak{J}\sh{L}$
is an ample $\sh{O}_{X_0}$-module.  Then $\mathfrak{X}$ is
algebraizable, and if $X$ is a proper $S$-scheme such
that $\mathfrak{X}$ is isomorphic to $\widehat{X}$, there exists an ample
$\sh{O}_X$-module $\sh{M}$ such that
$\sh{L}$ is isomorphic to $\widehat{\sh{M}}$ \textup{(which implies that
$X$ is \emph{projective} over $S$)}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let us apply \sref{III.5.2.3} to
$\sh{F}=\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}$: there therefore exists an integer $n_0$ such
that, for $n\geq n_0$, the canonical homomorphism $\Gamma(\mathfrak{X},\sh{L}^{\otimes n})
\to
\Gamma(X_0,\sh{L}_0^{\otimes n})$
is \emph{surjective}.  We may suppose $n\geq n_0$ chosen large enough that
$\sh{L}_0^{\otimes n}$ is \emph{very ample} for $S_0$
\sref[II]{II.4.5.10}.  Since the morphism
$f_0:X_0\to S_0$ is proper,
$\Gamma(X_0,\sh{L}_0^{\otimes n})$ is an $A$-module of finite type
\sref{III.3.2.1}, so there exists a sub-$A$-module of finite type $E$ of
$\Gamma(\mathfrak{X},\sh{L}^{\otimes n})$ whose image in
$\Gamma(X_0,\sh{L}_0^{\otimes n})$ is the whole of the latter module.
This being so, for every $k\geq0$, consider the homomorphism
$u_k:E/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}E\longrightarrow
\Gamma(X_k,\sh{L}_k^{\otimes n})$
deduced from the canonical injection
$E\to\Gamma(\mathfrak{X},\sh{L}^{\otimes n})$.  Note that
$(f_k)_*(\sh{L}_k^{\otimes n})$ is quasi-coherent, and since
$\Gamma(S_k,(f_k)_*(\sh{L}_k^{\otimes n}))
=\Gamma(X_k,\sh{L}_k^{\otimes n})$, $u_k$ defines a homomorphism
$\widetilde{u}_k:(E/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}E)^\sim
\longrightarrow(f_k)_*(\sh{L}_k^{\otimes n})$,
and consequently also a homomorphism
$\widetilde{u}_k^{\,\sharp}:
f_k^*((E/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}E)^\sim)\longrightarrow
\sh{L}_k^{\otimes n}$.
Moreover, if we put
$\sh{G}_k=f_k^*((E/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}E)^\sim)$, we have
$\sh{G}_0=\sh{G}_k/\mathfrak{J}\sh{G}_k$ \sref[I]{I.9.1.5}, so
$\widetilde{u}_0^{\,\sharp}:\sh{G}_k/\mathfrak{J}\sh{G}_k
\to\sh{L}_k^{\otimes n}/\mathfrak{J}\sh{L}_k^{\otimes n}$ is deduced
from $\widetilde{u}_k^{\,\sharp}$ by passage to the quotients.  Now, by the
definition of $E$, $\widetilde{u}_0^{\,\sharp}$ is none other than the
canonical homomorphism
$\sigma:f_0^*((f_0)_*(\sh{L}_0^{\otimes n}))\longrightarrow
\sh{L}_0^{\otimes n}$,
and the hypothesis that $\sh{L}_0^{\otimes n}$ is very ample implies that
$\widetilde{u}_0^{\,\sharp}$ is \emph{surjective} \sref[II]{II.4.4.3}; it then
follows from Nakayama's lemma that each of the $\widetilde{u}_k^{\,\sharp}$ is
also \emph{surjective}.
Each of the $\widetilde{u}_k^{\,\sharp}$ therefore defines \sref[II]{II.4.2.2} an
$S_k$-morphism
$g_k:X_k\longrightarrow P_k=\mathbf{P}(E/\mathfrak{J}^{k+1}E)$,
and since $P_h=P_k\times_{S_k}S_h$ for $h\leq k$ by virtue of \sref[II]{II.4.1.3},
$(g_k)$ is an $(S_k)$-\emph{adic inductive system} \sref[I]{I.10.12.2} by virtue of
the relations between the $\widetilde{u}_k^{\,\sharp}$ and of \sref[II]{II.4.2.10}.
The $g_k$ therefore define an $\mathfrak{S}$-morphism of formal schemes
$g:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{P}$, where $\mathfrak{P}$ is the inductive limit of
the system $(P_k)$, or again the completion $\widehat{P}$, where
$P=\mathbf{P}(E)$.  Moreover, the hypothesis that $\sh{L}_0^{\otimes n}$ is very
ample implies that $g_0$ is a \emph{closed immersion} \sref[II]{II.4.4.3}; we
conclude that $g$ is a closed immersion of formal schemes \sref{III.4.8.10},
so $\mathfrak{X}$ is algebraizable \sref{III.5.1.8}.  The fact that
$\sh{L}$ is isomorphic to the completion $\widehat{\sh{M}}$ of an invertible
$\sh{O}_X$-module then follows from the existence theorem \sref{III.5.1.6}.
Moreover, $\sh{L}^{\otimes n}$ is then the completion of
$\sh{M}^{\otimes n}$ \sref[I]{I.10.8.10}, and the homomorphisms
$\widetilde{u}_k^{\,\sharp}$ define a well-determined homomorphism
$v:f^*(\widetilde{E})\longrightarrow\sh{M}^{\otimes n}$
\sref{III.5.1.7}; moreover, since $\widetilde{u}_0^{\,\sharp}$ is surjective,
so is $\widehat{v}$ \sref[I]{I.10.11.5}, hence so is $v$ \sref{III.5.1.3};
furthermore, the morphism $r:X\to P$ defined by $v$ \sref[II]{II.4.2.2} has
$g$ as its extension to the completions, and since $g$ is a closed immersion,
so is $r$, by \sref{III.5.1.8} and \sref{III.5.4.1}; we conclude that
$\sh{M}^{\otimes n}$ is very ample \sref[II]{II.4.4.6} and $\sh{M}$ is ample
\sref[II]{II.4.5.10}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[5.4.6]
\label{III.5.4.6}
Let $A$ be an adic Noetherian ring, put
$\mathfrak{S}=\Spf(A)$, and let
$f:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{S}$ be a proper morphism of formal
schemes.  Let $\sh{N}$ be the coherent ideal of
$\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{X}}$ such that, for every affine formal open
subset $U$ of $\mathfrak{X}$, $\Gamma(U,\sh{N})$
is the nilradical of
$\Gamma(U,\sh{O}_{\mathfrak{X}})$.  The existence of this ideal
follows readily from \sref[I]{I.10.10.2} and from the fact that
every ring homomorphism $B\to C$ sends the nilradical of $B$ into
that of $C$.  Let $\mathfrak{X}'$ be the closed formal
subscheme of $\mathfrak{X}$ defined by $\sh{N}$
\sref[I]{I.10.14.2}; it would be interesting to know whether,
when $\mathfrak{X}'$ is algebraizable, $\mathfrak{X}$ itself is
algebraizable.  One would doubtless arrive at a solution of this
problem if one knew how to classify (for example by means of invariants
----- English, ega3/ega3-6.tex, page III 158 -----
\oldpage[III]{158}

Choose finite affine open coverings $\mathfrak U^{(i)}$ of
$X^{(i)}$, and let $\mathfrak U^{\prime(i)}$ be their traces on
$X^{\prime(i)}$.  The latter are again coverings by affine open
subsets \sref[I]{I.5.5.10}, and more precisely
\[
  C^\bullet
  \bigl(
    \mathfrak U^{\prime(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{\prime(i)}
  \bigr)
  =
  C^\bullet
  \bigl(
    \mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)}
  \bigr)
  \otimes_{B_i}(B_i)_{g_i}.
\]
Put
\[
  L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}
  =
  C^\bullet
  \bigl(
    \mathfrak U^{(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{(i)}
  \bigr),
  \qquad
  L_{\bullet\bullet}^{\prime(i)}
  =
  C^\bullet
  \bigl(
    \mathfrak U^{\prime(i)},\sh P_\bullet^{\prime(i)}
  \bigr).
\]
Then
\[
\begin{split}
  L_{\bullet\bullet}^{\prime(1)}
  \otimes_A L_{\bullet\bullet}^{\prime(2)}
  &=
  \bigl(
    L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}
    \otimes_{B_1}(B_1)_{g_1}
  \bigr)
  \otimes_A
  \bigl(
    L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}
    \otimes_{B_2}(B_2)_{g_2}
  \bigr).
\end{split}
\]
Since $B_g=(B_1)_{g_1}\otimes_A(B_2)_{g_2}$
up to canonical isomorphism, we likewise have, up to canonical
isomorphism, $L_{\bullet\bullet}^{\prime(1)}
\otimes_A L_{\bullet\bullet}^{\prime(2)}
=
\bigl(
L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}
\otimes_A L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}
\bigr)
\otimes_BB_g$.

Let $M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}$ be a projective
Cartan--Eilenberg resolution of $L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}$,
chosen to consist of $B_i$-modules.  Because $(B_i)_{g_i}$ is
flat over $B_i$, $M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{\prime(i)}
=
M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}
\otimes_{B_i}(B_i)_{g_i}$
is a projective Cartan--Eilenberg resolution of
$L_{\bullet\bullet}^{\prime(i)}$.  Moreover,
\[
  M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{\prime(1)}
  \otimes_A M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{\prime(2)}
  =
  \bigl(
    M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}
    \otimes_A M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \otimes_BB_g.
\]
The desired isomorphism \eqref{III.6.7.1.1.eq} now follows
immediately from the definitions of the hyperhomology of a
bicomplex and from the exactness of
$G\longmapsto G\otimes_BB_g$ on $B$-modules.
\end{env}

\begin{env}[6.7.2]
\label{III.6.7.2}
Now suppose that $S$ is arbitrary, and let
\[
  u_i:Y^{(i)}\longrightarrow S
  \qquad(i=1,2)
\]
be the structural morphisms.  Choose an affine open covering
$(S_\alpha)$ of $S$, put
\[
  Y_\alpha^{(i)}=u_i^{-1}(S_\alpha),
  \qquad
  X_\alpha^{(i)}=f_i^{-1}(Y_\alpha^{(i)}),
\]
and let $f_{i\alpha}:X_\alpha^{(i)}\longrightarrow Y_\alpha^{(i)}$
be the restriction of $f_i$, which is separated and
quasi-compact.  The schemes $Y_\alpha
=
Y_\alpha^{(1)}
\times_{S_\alpha}Y_\alpha^{(2)}$
form an open covering of $Y$, and on every $Y_\alpha$,
\sref{III.6.7.1} defines the spectral functors
\[
  {}^{(t)}\sh E^{S_\alpha}
  \left(
    f_{1\alpha},f_{2\alpha};
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)}|X_\alpha^{(1)},
    \sh P_\bullet^{(2)}|X_\alpha^{(2)}
  \right).
\]
It remains again to show that these functors satisfy the gluing
conditions.

We reduce immediately to the following situation:
$S=\Spec(A)$ is affine,
$S'=D(h)$ with $h\in A$, and
$u_i(Y^{(i)})\subset S'$.  We may moreover assume that
$Y^{(i)}=\Spec(B_i)$ is affine.  It is then enough to define
canonical isomorphisms
\begin{env}[6.7.2.1]
\label{III.6.7.2.1}
\[
  {}^{(t)}E^S
  \bigl(
    X^{(1)},X^{(2)};
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \xrightarrow{\sim}
  {}^{(t)}E^{S'}
  \bigl(
    X^{(1)},X^{(2)};
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr).
  \tag{6.7.2.1}\label{III.6.7.2.1.eq}
\]
\end{env}
With the notation of \sref{III.6.6.2}, the
$L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}$ consist of $A_h$-modules, and hence,
up to canonical isomorphism, $L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}
\otimes_{A_h}L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}
=
L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}
\otimes_A L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}$.
Since a projective Cartan--Eilenberg resolution
$M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}$ of $L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(i)}$ may be
chosen to consist of $A_h$-modules, the desired canonical
isomorphism follows immediately.
\end{env}

\subsection{A decomposition of certain schemes}
\label{subsection:III.5.5}

\begin{proposition}[5.5.1]
\label{III.5.5.1}
Let $A$ be an adic Noetherian ring, let $\mathfrak{J}$ be an ideal
of definition of $A$, and put $Y=\Spec(A)$.  Let $f:X\to Y$ be a
separated morphism of finite type, and put
\[
  Y_0=\Spec(A/\mathfrak{J}),\qquad
  X_0=X\times_Y Y_0=f^{-1}(Y_0).
\]
Let $Z_0$ be an open subset of $X_0$ which is proper over $Y_0$.
Then there is an open and closed subset $Z$ of $X$, proper over
$Y$, such that $Z\cap X_0=Z_0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By hypothesis, there is an open subset $T$ of $X$ such that
$T\cap X_0=Z_0$; let $\widehat{T}$ be the completion along $Z_0$
of the scheme induced by $X$ on the open set $T$; since the support of
$\sh{O}_{\widehat{T}}$ is $Z_0$, which is proper over $Y_0$,
$\widehat{T}$ is proper over
$\widehat{Y}=\Spf(A)$ \sref{III.3.4.1}.  It follows from \sref{III.5.1.8}
that there exists a closed subscheme $Z$ of $T$, \emph{proper} over $Y$,
such that, if $i:Z\to T$ is the canonical injection,
$\widehat{i}:\widehat{Z}\to\widehat{T}$ is an isomorphism
($\widehat{Z}$ being the completion of $Z$ along $Z_0$).  We conclude
\sref{III.4.6.8} that there exists in $T$ an open neighbourhood $V$ of
$Z_0$ such that the restriction $i^{-1}(V)\to V$ of $i$ is an
isomorphism.  But $i^{-1}(V)$ is a neighbourhood of $Z_0$ in $Z$, and
is therefore necessarily identical to $Z$ \sref{III.5.1.3.1}.  We
conclude that $Z$ is open in $T$, hence in $X$, which completes the
proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[5.5.2]
\label{III.5.5.2}
If $X_0$ is proper over $Y_0$, then $X$ is the union of two
disjoint open subsets $Z$ and $Z'$ such that $Z$ is proper over
$Y$ and contains $X_0$.  Moreover, every closed subset $P$ of $X$
which is proper over $Y$ is contained in $Z$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The last assertion follows from the fact that $P\cap Z'$, being closed
in $P$, is proper over $Y$; if $P\cap Z'$ were not empty, $f(P\cap Z')$
would be closed and nonempty in $Y$, hence would meet $Y_0$
\sref{III.5.1.3.1}, which contradicts the definition of $Z$.
\end{proof}

\emph{(To be continued.)}
\end{flushright}

\clearpage
----- English, ega3/ega3-6.tex, page III 159 -----
\oldpage[III]{159}
with values in the category of quasi-coherent
$\sh O_Y$-modules, such that if $V^{(i)}$ is an affine open subset of
$Y^{(i)}$ $(i=1,2)$ and
$V=V^{(1)}\times_SV^{(2)}$, we have
\[
\begin{split}
  &\boldsymbol{\mathscr{T}\!or}_\bullet^S
  \bigl(
    f_1,f_2;
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)|V\\
  &\quad=
  \left(
    \mathbf{Tor}_\bullet^S
    \left(
      f_1^{-1}(V^{(1)}),f_2^{-1}(V^{(2)});
      \sh P_\bullet^{(1)}|f_1^{-1}(V^{(1)}),
      \sh P_\bullet^{(2)}|f_2^{-1}(V^{(2)})
    \right)
  \right)\supertilde .
\end{split}
\]

This bifunctor is the abutment of six biregular spectral
functors
\[
  {}^{(t)}\sh E
  \bigl(
    f_1,f_2;
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \qquad(t=a,b,a',b',c,d),
\]
whose $E_2$ terms are given by
\[
\begin{aligned}
  {}^{(a)}\sh E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \bigoplus_{q_1+q_2=q}
  \shTor_p^S
  \bigl(
    \mathcal{H}^{-q_1}(f_1,\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}),
    \mathcal{H}^{-q_2}(f_2,\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr),\\
  {}^{(b)}\sh E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \sh H^{-p}
  \bigl(
    f_1\times_Sf_2,
    \boldsymbol{\mathscr{T}\!or}_q^S
    (\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr),\\
  {}^{(a')}\sh E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \bigoplus_{q_1+q_2=q}
  \boldsymbol{\mathscr{T}\!or}_p^S
  \bigl(
    \sh H^{-q_1}(f_1,\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}),
    \sh H^{-q_2}(f_2,\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr),\\
  {}^{(b')}\sh E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \mathcal{H}^{-p}
  \bigl(
    f_1\times_Sf_2,
    \shTor_q^S
    (\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr),\\
  {}^{(c)}\sh E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \bigoplus_{q_1+q_2=q}
  \mathscr{T}\!or_p^S
  \bigl(
    f_1,f_2;
    \sh H_{q_1}(\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}),
    \sh H_{q_2}(\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \bigr),\\
  {}^{(d)}\sh E_{pq}^2
  &=
  \sh H_p
  \left(
    \mathscr{T}\!or_q^S
    \bigl(
      f_1,f_2;
      \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
    \bigr)
  \right).
\end{aligned}
\]
\end{theorem}
\noindent The spectral sequences $(a)$ and $(b)$ are
called the \emph{K\"unneth spectral sequences}.

Note that the spectral sequences
$(a)$ and $(a')$ (resp. $(b)$ and $(b')$) are identical when
$\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}$ and $\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}$ reduce to their terms
of degree $0$; in that case, the sequences $(c)$ and
$(d)$ are degenerate and are therefore of no
interest.

\begin{remark}[6.7.4]
\label{III.6.7.4}
The global hypertors defined above include as special cases both
the \emph{Modules of hypercohomology} defined in \sref{III.6.2.1} and the
\emph{local hypertors} defined in \sref{III.6.5.3}.  Let us show that, for every
\emph{quasi-compact} and \emph{separated} morphism $f:X\to Y$ and every
bounded-below complex $\sh P_\bullet$ of quasi-coherent
$\sh O_X$-modules, there is a canonical isomorphism of
$\partial$-functors in $\sh P_\bullet$
\phantomsection\egalabel{III.6.7.4.1}{6.7.4.1}
\[
  \boldsymbol{\mathscr{T}\!or}_n^Y
  \bigl(
    f,1_Y;
    \sh P_\bullet,\sh O_Y
  \bigr)
  \xrightarrow{\sim}
  \mathcal{H}^{-n}(f,\sh P_\bullet)
  \qquad\text{(for every $n\in\mathbf Z$)}.
  \tag{6.7.4.1}\label{III.6.7.4.1.eq}
\]

Indeed, the gluing methods of \sref{III.6.7.2} immediately reduce the
proof to the case in which $Y$ is affine.  By
\sref{III.6.2.2}, both sides of
\eqref{III.6.7.4.1.eq} may then be computed using the same
finite covering $\mathfrak U$ of $X$ by affine open subsets and,
for the first side, the covering of $Y$ consisting of $Y$
itself.  With the notation of \sref{III.6.6.2}, the bicomplex
$L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}$ is then concentrated in bidegree
$(0,0)$, where it is equal to $A$, and the conclusion follows
from (\textbf{0},~11.7.5).

For a generalization, see \sref{III.6.7.7}.  Notice, however,
that if $\sh O_Y$ on the left side of
\eqref{III.6.7.4.1.eq} is replaced by an \emph{arbitrary}
quasi-coherent $\sh O_Y$-module $\sh F$, there is in general no
longer an isomorphism with $\mathcal{H}^{-n}
\bigl(
f,\sh P_\bullet\otimes_{\sh O_Y}\sh F
\bigr)$,
although the bicomplex
$L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}\otimes_A
 L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}$ occurring in the preceding
calculation still identifies with $C^\bullet
\bigl(
\mathfrak U,
\sh P_\bullet\otimes_Y\sh F
\bigr)$.

On the other hand, there is a canonical isomorphism of
bi-$\partial$-functors
\phantomsection\egalabel{III.6.7.4.2}{6.7.4.2}
\[
  \boldsymbol{\mathscr{T}\!or}_\bullet^S
  \bigl(
    1_{X^{(1)}},1_{X^{(2)}};
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \xrightarrow{\sim}
  \boldsymbol{\shTor}_\bullet^S
  \bigl(
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr).
  \tag{6.7.4.2}\label{III.6.7.4.2.eq}
\]

Indeed, \sref{III.6.7.1} and \sref{III.6.7.2} reduce us again to
the case in which $S$ and the $X^{(i)}$ are affine.  In
computing the first side of \eqref{III.6.7.4.2.eq}, we may then
take $\mathfrak U^{(i)}$ to consist of the single member
$X^{(i)}$.

immersion fermée de schémas formels (4.8.10), donc $\mathfrak X$ est
algébrisable (5.1.8). Le fait que $\mathcal L$ soit isomorphe au complété
$\widehat{\mathcal M}$ d'un $\mathcal O_X$-Module inversible ré

\begin{proposition}[6.7.5]
\label{III.6.7.5}
Let $u:\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}
\longrightarrow
\sh Q_\bullet^{(1)}$
be a homomorphism of bounded-below complexes of quasi-coherent
$\sh O_{X^{(1)}}$-modules such that the induced homomorphism
\[
  \sh H_\bullet(u):
  \sh H_\bullet(\sh P_\bullet^{(1)})
  \longrightarrow
  \sh H_\bullet(\sh Q_\bullet^{(1)})
\]
is an isomorphism.  Then the homomorphisms
\[
  {}^{(t)}\sh E
  \bigl(
    f_1,f_2;
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \longrightarrow
  {}^{(t)}\sh E
  \bigl(
    f_1,f_2;
    \sh Q_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
\]
induced by $u$ are isomorphisms for $t=a,b,c$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The assertion concerning the spectral sequence $(c)$ follows
from the fact that this sequence is biregular and that, by hypothesis, the
homomorphism under consideration is an isomorphism for the
$E_2$ terms \sref[0]{0.11.1.5}.  This already shows that
\[
\begin{split}
  \boldsymbol{\mathscr{T}\!or}_\bullet^S
  \bigl(
    f_1,f_2;
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \longrightarrow
  \boldsymbol{\mathscr{T}\!or}_\bullet^S
  \bigl(
    f_1,f_2;
    \sh Q_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
\end{split}
\]
is an isomorphism.  Applying \eqref{III.6.7.4.1.eq} and
\eqref{III.6.7.4.2.eq}, we first see that the homomorphisms $\mathcal{H}^{-n}(f_1,\sh P_\bullet^{(1)})
\longrightarrow
\mathcal{H}^{-n}(f_1,\sh Q_\bullet^{(1)})$
and $\boldsymbol{\shTor}_n^S
\bigl(
\sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
\bigr)
\longrightarrow
\boldsymbol{\shTor}_n^S
\bigl(
\sh Q_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
\bigr)$
induced by $u$ are isomorphisms.  The assertion concerning the sequences
$(a)$ and $(b)$ then follows from the fact that these sequences are
biregular \sref{III.6.7.3} and that the homomorphisms considered are
bijective for the $E_2$ terms \sref[0]{0.11.1.5}.

Note on the other hand that if
$u:\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}\to\sh Q_\bullet^{(1)}$ is a
\emph{homotopism}, one deduces from it canonical isomorphisms
\[
  {}^{(t)}\sh E
  \bigl(
    f_1,f_2;
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \longrightarrow
  {}^{(t)}\sh E
  \bigl(
    f_1,f_2;
    \sh Q_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
\]
for the six spectral sequences.  Indeed, if $S$ and the
$Y^{(i)}$ are affine, one deduces from $u$ a homotopism of
bicomplexes $C^\bullet
\bigl(
\mathfrak U^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}
\bigr)
\longrightarrow
C^\bullet
\bigl(
\mathfrak U^{(1)},\sh Q_\bullet^{(1)}
\bigr)$,
and the proposition follows from the general theory of
hyperhomology \sref[0]{0.11.3.2}; the passage to the general
case is made by gluing, using the fact that from a homotopism of
complexes one deduces a homotopism of projective
Cartan--Eilenberg resolutions of these complexes
(\textnormal{M}, XVII, 1.2).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[6.7.6]
\label{III.6.7.6}
Suppose that the complex $\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}$ or the complex
$\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}$ consists of $S$-flat modules (both
complexes being bounded below).  There is then a canonical
isomorphism of bi-$\partial$-functors
\phantomsection\egalabel{III.6.7.6.1}{6.7.6.1}
\[
  \boldsymbol{\mathscr{T}\!or}_n^S
  \bigl(
    f_1,f_2;
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \xrightarrow{\sim}
  \mathcal{H}^{-n}
  \bigl(
    f_1\times_Sf_2,
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)}
    \otimes_S\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr).
  \tag{6.7.6.1}\label{III.6.7.6.1.eq}
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
First suppose that $S$, $Y^{(1)}$, and $Y^{(2)}$ are affine, so
that we are in the situation of \sref{III.6.6.2}, whose notation
we retain.  Suppose, for example, that
$\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}$ consists of $S$-flat modules, and let us compute
the hypertor using the remark \sref{III.6.6.6}: it is thus the homology of $L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}
\otimes_A M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}$,
where $M_{\bullet\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}$ is a projective
Cartan--Eilenberg resolution of $L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}$ in
the sense of \sref[0]{0.11.7.1}.  On the other hand, the modules
$L_{ij}^{(1)}$ are \emph{flat} over $A$ by virtue of the hypothesis
\sref{III.1.4.15.1}; one then deduces from
(\textbf{0},~11.7.5) a canonical isomorphism
\phantomsection\egalabel{III.6.7.6.2}{6.7.6.2}
\[
  \mathbf{Tor}_\bullet^S
  \bigl(
    \mathfrak U^{(1)},\mathfrak U^{(2)};
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \xrightarrow{\sim}
  \sh H_\bullet
  \bigl(
    L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}
    \otimes_A L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}
  \bigr).
  \tag{6.7.6.2}\label{III.6.7.6.2.eq}
\]

On the other hand, there is a natural homomorphism of bicomplexes
of $L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(1)}\otimes_A L_{\bullet\bullet}^{(2)}$ into
$C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\sh Q_\bullet)$,
where $\mathfrak U$ is the covering of $Z=X^{(1)}\times_SX^{(2)}$
by the affine open subsets

\end{remark}

cochains \sref[0]{0.11.8.7}.  Moreover, the homomorphism of

\sref{III.6.6.4}; composing with

\phantomsection\egalabel{III.6.7.6.3}{6.7.6.3}

depend on the open coverings chosen (the right side of
\eqref{III.6.7.6.3.eq} being canonically isomorphic to $\mathbf{H}^{-n}
\bigl(

notation of \sref{III.6.6.7}) that we have a diagram that is commutative up to
homotopisms
\[
\xymatrix@C=18pt@R=28pt{
  C^\bullet(\mathfrak U^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(1)})
  \otimes_A
  C^\bullet(\mathfrak U^{(2)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \ar[r]\ar[d]
  &
  C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\sh Q_\bullet)
  \ar[d]
  \\
  C^\bullet(\mathfrak V^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(1)})
  \otimes_A
  C^\bullet(\mathfrak V^{(2)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})
  \ar[r]
  &
  C^\bullet(\mathfrak V,\sh Q_\bullet)
}
\]
where the horizontal arrows are the homomorphisms defined above.  Finally, one
must pass to the general case by gluing, which is done without difficulty as in
\sref{III.6.7.1} and \sref{III.6.7.2}; we leave the details to
the reader.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[6.7.7]
\label{III.6.7.7}
Suppose that $\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}$ and
$\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}$ are bounded below, and that either all the
modules $\mathcal{H}^{-n}(f_1,\sh P_\bullet^{(1)})$
or all the modules $\mathcal{H}^{-n}(f_2,\sh P_\bullet^{(2)})$
are $S$-flat.  There is then a canonical isomorphism of
bi-$\partial$-functors, for $n\in\mathbf Z$,
\begin{env}[6.7.7.1]
\label{III.6.7.7.1}
\[
  \boldsymbol{\mathscr{T}\!or}_n^S
  \bigl(
    f_1,f_2;
    \sh P_\bullet^{(1)},\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}
  \bigr)
  \xrightarrow{\sim}
  \bigoplus_{q_1+q_2=n}
  \mathcal{H}^{-q_1}(f_1,\sh P_\bullet^{(1)})
  \otimes_S
  \mathcal{H}^{-q_2}(f_2,\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}).
  \tag{6.7.7.1}\label{III.6.7.7.1.eq}
\]
\end{env}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By \sref{III.6.5.8}, spectral sequence \textnormal{(a)} of
\sref{III.6.7.3} degenerates.  The assertion therefore follows
immediately from \sref[0]{0.11.1.6}, since that spectral sequence
is biregular \sref{III.6.7.3}.
\end{proof}

    $\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}$ are bounded below.
  \item[{$2^\circ$}]
    Either $\sh P_\bullet^{(1)}$ or
    $\sh P_\bullet^{(2)}$ consists of $S$-flat modules.
